I’m Dr. [Your Name]. What brings you in today? Is there something specific you’d like to talk about or get help with?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. I... well, I came in because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. It's slipping. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program... I've been helping out there, you know. They do a lot of robotics and woodworking. My grandfather was a carpenter, actually. He built half the cabinets in my childhood home with his bare hands. The wood was this beautiful cherry oak. You just don't see craftsmanship like that anymore, everything now is just particle board from IKEA... but, um... where was I? \n\nRight, the note. I try to write a simple note for the program, something I could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. They just scatter. I can't hold them.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these memory problems? Were they sudden or did they come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been about three weeks now. I know exactly when it started because... well, it was right after my older sister passed away. Three weeks ago. She was the one who taught me how to read, actually. We used to sit on the porch of that same house with the cherry oak cabinets, and she'd point out the letters in the Sunday paper. She loved the comics. Peanuts, mostly. I always preferred Calvin and Hobbes, but she said Calvin was too loud. She was always so quiet, you know? Never raised her voice. Not even when she got sick at the end. She just... faded. Like an old photograph left out in the sun. I have a lot of photographs from those days, mostly in black and white. Color film was expensive back then, and my father didn't believe in wasting money on... wait. What were we talking about? The memory thing, right? Yes, about three weeks. Ever since she died.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory besides forgetting things? For example, feeling confused, getting lost in familiar places, or having trouble finding the right words when you speak?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? No... no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I know my way around just fine. I still walk down to the corner market, the same route I've taken for thirty years. It used to be owned by the Miller brothers. Good people. Now it's one of those big corporate chains with the bright fluorescent lights that just drain the life right out of you. \n\nThey rearranged all the aisles a few weeks ago, which is frustrating. I was just looking for coffee filters. Just standard basket filters, but they moved them next to the herbal tea. My sister drank tea. Chamomile, usually. She said coffee made her too jittery. We had this old stovetop kettle that whistled so loud... it had a little copper bird on the spout. I haven't heard that whistle in years. I think it got packed away in the attic when I retired. The attic is just full of boxes now. I was an accountant, so you can imagine the paperwork I kept. I used to be so sharp with all those details, balancing everything to the penny... \n\nUm. I'm sorry. What did you ask me? Was it about the grocery store?\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble following conversations, thinking clearly, or keeping track of tasks you need to do?\n\nPatient: Following conversations... yes, that's exactly it. That's what I was trying to say earlier about the youth maker program. I just can't think clearly anymore. I'll sit down to write a simple note for the kids—something I used to do in my sleep—and the numbers and the words, they just scatter. They scatter like... like leaves in the wind. \n\nWe used to have this massive oak tree in the front yard when I was a boy. Every autumn, the yard would just be buried in these crisp, brown leaves. My father would make me rake them into these huge piles. I hated doing it, but I loved jumping into them afterward. The smell of those dry leaves... it's a very specific smell, isn't it? Earthy and kind of sharp. My sister would always scold me because I'd get bits of leaf stuck in my hair, and she was the one who had to help me pick them out before dinner. We always had dinner at exactly six o'clock. Roast beef on Sundays, meatloaf on Tuesdays... my mother was very rigid about the schedule. She believed structure was the key to a good life. But now... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? The scattering. What was the question? Something about keeping track of things?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy, like the youth maker program or anything else?\n\nPatient: Interest? Well, yes, I suppose. The youth maker program... I haven't even touched the wood pieces on my bench in weeks. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. Dust is funny, isn't it? It just settles everywhere. My mother used to have this feather duster, a real ostrich feather one. She'd go around the house every Saturday morning, flicking it over the lampshades and the picture frames. The dust would just swirl up into the sunlight streaming through the windows... it looked like tiny stars dancing. We had these heavy velvet curtains in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were awful to clean. You had to take them down and haul them to the dry cleaner across town. The man who ran it, Mr. Abernathy, always had a piece of hard candy for me. Butterscotch, usually. I never really liked butterscotch, but I took it to be polite. It's important to be polite, don't you think? \n\nBut yes, the wood pieces... and I've skipped the choir planning committee, too. And my chess games. Even fishing... I used to love going down to the lake early in the morning when the mist was still on the water. But now, it just sounds like so much effort. More effort than it's worth. Which isn't like me at all. I used to be so active. Always doing something. But now... I'm sorry, did that answer your question about the fishing?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day, since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Sad... yes, I suppose that's the word. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It's just there, the minute I open my eyes. And even when my wife is talking kindly to me, I feel like I'm sitting at the bottom of a well. Just looking up from the dark, completely disconnected. \n\nWe actually had an old stone well on the property where I grew up. My grandfather built it. He laid every single stone by hand. He had these massive, rough hands... he was a stonemason for forty years. He helped build the old courthouse downtown. The one with the big copper clock tower. They don't make buildings like that anymore, with the gargoyles and the real marble floors. Whenever you walked into that courthouse, your footsteps would echo all the way up to the ceiling. I had to go there once for jury duty in the eighties. It was a case about a stolen tractor, if you can believe it. A bright green John Deere. The farmer who owned it was so red in the face when he testified... he wore these thick striped suspenders. You don't see people wearing suspenders much these days, do you? Usually just belts. I have a nice brown leather belt my sister bought me for my birthday a few years ago. She always gave the most practical gifts... \n\nUm. I'm sorry. I'm drifting again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about my wife?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level lately? Like feeling more tired than usual or finding it hard to get through the day?\n\nPatient: Energy? I don't know... I'm not really sure if my energy is different, to be honest. I mean, I suppose I just sit in my chair most of the day now. It's a very comfortable chair, actually. A La-Z-Boy recliner. My wife bought it for me when I retired. It's this deep forest green color, a sort of plush fabric that's incredibly soft. \n\nGreen has always been my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind my childhood home. We used to spend hours back there, my sister and I, building forts out of fallen branches and pretending we were explorers. We'd pack a lunch in an old tin pail—usually peanut butter and jelly sandwiches, cut into triangles, because my sister insisted they tasted better that way. She was very particular about things like that. Even when we were older, she always had to have her tea in a specific floral teacup. It was part of a set she found at an antique shop in Vermont. We took a trip up there one October just to see the foliage. The colors were spectacular... reds and oranges so bright they almost hurt your eyes. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman who made the most incredible blueberry muffins. She said the secret was a dash of nutmeg in the batter. I tried making them once when I got home, but they just didn't turn out the same. Baking is such a precise science, isn't it? You have to measure everything perfectly, or it all falls flat. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask about the chair? I haven't really noticed feeling tired, just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, yes, I suppose that's true. Everything I do has slowed down. Even just getting out of that green chair I mentioned... it takes me forever. It's like my body is moving through wet cement. Even answering your questions feels like my tongue is stuck in cement. \n\nCement is funny stuff, though. You have to mix it just right. My neighbor, Tom, he tried to pour his own patio last summer. Rented a mixer and everything. He didn't get the ratio of water to powder right, and the whole thing cracked right down the middle after the first frost. Frost heaves are terrible around here. They ruin the roads every winter. The city comes around with those patch trucks, throwing cold patch into the potholes, but it never holds. You drive down Elm Street in March, and it's like navigating a minefield. I lost a hubcap on Elm Street once. A shiny chrome one off my old Buick LeSabre. That was a wonderful car. It floated down the highway like a boat. It had those bench seats in the front... you don't see bench seats in cars anymore. Everyone wants bucket seats and center consoles now. My daughter's car has a console that cools your drinks. Can you imagine? A refrigerator right there in the armrest. She always keeps those little cans of sparkling water in it. The grapefruit flavor. It's very tart... \n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? Were we talking about Tom's patio?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or felt things that others don’t seem to notice? Or have you believed something strongly that others say isn’t true?\n\nPatient: Seen or heard things? No... no, nothing like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. My hearing is actually quite good for my age, though. Better than my wife's, anyway. She always has the television turned up so loud. She watches those British mystery shows on PBS on Sunday nights. The ones where they live in those grand old manor houses with the sprawling gardens. \n\nI've always wanted a garden like that, with the manicured hedges and the rose bushes. My mother used to grow roses. She had a whole trellis of climbing yellow roses by the back porch. They smelled wonderful in the summer, especially right after a rainstorm. The rain would leave these perfect little droplets on the petals. I used to try and catch the droplets on my finger when I was a boy. We had this big oak tree in the yard, too, and my father hung a tire swing from one of the thickest branches. He used a thick, braided rope he bought from the hardware store downtown. Old Mr. Henderson owned that store. He kept jars of hard candies on the counter for the kids. Butterscotch and peppermint. I always chose the butterscotch. They were wrapped in that crinkly yellow cellophane that made a sharp noise when you twisted it open. It's funny how a sound like that stays with you, isn't it? The crinkle of a candy wrapper. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Something about the television being too loud?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling slowed down or struggling to think clearly, like your thoughts are moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Slowed down... yes. My mind, it's just... I can't seem to hold onto things anymore. That's actually why I came in. I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the youth maker program I volunteer with. It's something I could do in my sleep before, just jotting down a few instructions. But the numbers and words, they just scattered on me. Like trying to catch leaves in the wind. \n\nSpeaking of leaves, the maple out front is already starting to drop its leaves. It seems early this year. Usually, we don't have to start raking until late October. I have this old bamboo rake I've used for decades. The metal ones just tear up the grass, you know? Bamboo is flexible. It glides over the lawn. My grandson, he tried to help me last fall, but he kept swinging the rake like a golf club. He's very into golf right now. Wants to be the next Tiger Woods. I tried playing golf once, back in the eighties. Bought a whole set of clubs at a yard sale. But I could never get the swing right. It always sliced to the right. Right into the woods. Every single time. We spent more time looking for lost balls than actually playing the game. \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? The youth program?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty thinking clearly feel different from how you normally experience grief or stress, and have they been affecting your daily life more than you would expect for someone going through something like this?\n\nPatient: Different? My wife and our pastor, they've had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. They don't even think it's safe for me to be out in my workshop by myself anymore. \n\nI have a beautiful workshop out back. Built it myself, board by board, back in '98. I used cedar for the siding. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. My grandmother used to have a cedar chest at the foot of her bed. She kept all the winter quilts in it. Now it's one of those big corporate chains with the bright fluorescent lights that just drain the life right out of you. \n\nThey rearranged all the aisles a few weeks ago, which is frustrating. We had this old stovetop kettle that whistled so loud... it had a little copper bird on the spout. I haven't heard that whistle in years. I think it got packed away in the attic when I retired. The attic is just full of boxes now. I was an accountant, so you can imagine the paperwork I kept. I used to be so sharp with all those details, balancing everything to the penny... \n\nUm. I'm sorry. What did you ask me? Was it about the grocery store?\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble following conversations, thinking clearly, or keeping track of tasks you need to do?\n\nPatient: Following conversations... yes, that's exactly it. That's what I was trying to say earlier about the youth maker program. I just can't think clearly anymore. I'll sit down to write a simple note for the kids—something I used to do in my sleep—and the numbers and the words, they just scatter. They scatter like... like leaves in the wind. \n\nWe used to have this massive oak tree in the front yard when I was a boy. Every autumn, the yard would just be buried in these crisp, brown leaves. My father would make me rake them into these huge piles. I hated doing it, but I loved jumping into them afterward. The smell of those dry leaves... it's a very specific smell, isn't it? Earthy and kind of sharp. My sister would always scold me because I'd get bits of leaf stuck in my hair, and she was the one who had to help me pick them out before dinner. We always had dinner at exactly six o'clock. Roast beef on Sundays, meatloaf on Tuesdays... my mother was very rigid about the schedule. She believed structure was the key to a good life. But now... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? The scattering. What was the question? Something about keeping track of things?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy, like the youth maker program or anything else?\n\nPatient: Interest? Well, yes, I suppose. The youth maker program... I haven't even touched the wood pieces on my bench in weeks. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. Dust is funny, isn't it? It just settles everywhere. My mother used to have this feather duster, a real ostrich feather one. She'd go around the house every Saturday morning, flicking it over the lampshades and the picture frames. The dust would just swirl up into the sunlight streaming through the windows... it looked like tiny stars dancing. We had these heavy velvet curtains in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were awful to clean. You had to take them down and haul them to the dry cleaner across town. The man who ran it, Mr. Abernathy, always had a piece of hard candy for me. Butterscotch, usually. I never really liked butterscotch, but I took it to be polite. It's important to be polite, don't you think? \n\nBut yes, the wood pieces... and I've skipped the choir planning committee, too. And my chess games. Even fishing... I used to love going down to the lake early in the morning when the mist was still on the water. But now, it just sounds like so much effort. More effort than it's worth. Which isn't like me at all. I used to be so active. Always doing something. But now... I'm sorry, did that answer your question about the fishing?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day, since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Sad... yes, I suppose that's the word. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It's just there, the minute I open my eyes. And even when my wife is talking kindly to me, I feel like I'm sitting at the bottom of a well. Just looking up from the dark, completely disconnected. \n\nWe actually had an old stone well on the property where I grew up. My grandfather built it. He laid every single stone by hand. He had these massive, rough hands... he was a stonemason for forty years. He helped build the old courthouse downtown. The one with the big copper clock tower. They don't make buildings like that anymore, with the gargoyles and the real marble floors. Whenever you walked into that courthouse, your footsteps would echo all the way up to the ceiling. I had to go there once for jury duty in the eighties. It was a case about a stolen tractor, if you can believe it. A bright green John Deere. The farmer who owned it was so red in the face when he testified... he wore these thick striped suspenders. You don't see people wearing suspenders much these days, do you? Usually just belts. I have a nice brown leather belt my sister bought me for my birthday a few years ago. She always gave the most practical gifts... \n\nUm. I'm sorry. I'm drifting again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about my wife?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level lately? Like feeling more tired than usual or finding it hard to get through the day?\n\nPatient: Energy? I don't know... I'm not really sure if my energy is different, to be honest. I mean, I suppose I just sit in my chair most of the day now. It's a very comfortable chair, actually. A La-Z-Boy recliner. My wife bought it for me when I retired. It's this deep forest green color, a sort of plush fabric that's incredibly soft. \n\nGreen has always been my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind my childhood home. We used to spend hours back there, my sister and I, building forts out of fallen branches and pretending we were explorers. We'd pack a lunch in an old tin pail—usually peanut butter and jelly sandwiches, cut into triangles, because my sister insisted they tasted better that way. She was very particular about things like that. Even when we were older, she always had to have her tea in a specific floral teacup. It was part of a set she found at an antique shop in Vermont. We took a trip up there one October just to see the foliage. The colors were spectacular... reds and oranges so bright they almost hurt your eyes. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman who made the most incredible blueberry muffins. She said the secret was a dash of nutmeg in the batter. I tried making them once when I got home, but they just didn't turn out the same. Baking is such a precise science, isn't it? You have to measure everything perfectly, or it all falls flat. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask about the chair? I haven't really noticed feeling tired, just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, yes, I suppose that's true. Everything I do has slowed down. Even just getting out of that green chair I mentioned... it takes me forever. It's like my body is moving through wet cement. Even answering your questions feels like my tongue is stuck in cement. \n\nCement is funny stuff, though. You have to mix it just right. My neighbor, Tom, he tried to pour his own patio last summer. Rented a mixer and everything. He didn't get the ratio of water to powder right, and the whole thing cracked right down the middle after the first frost. Frost heaves are terrible around here. They ruin the roads every winter. The city comes around with those patch trucks, throwing cold patch into the potholes, but it never holds. You drive down Elm Street in March, and it's like navigating a minefield. I lost a hubcap on Elm Street once. A shiny chrome one off my old Buick LeSabre. That was a wonderful car. It floated down the highway like a boat. It had those bench seats in the front... you don't see bench seats in cars anymore. Everyone wants bucket seats and center consoles now. My daughter's car has a console that cools your drinks. Can you imagine? A refrigerator right there in the armrest. She always keeps those little cans of sparkling water in it. The grapefruit flavor. It's very tart... \n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? Were we talking about Tom's patio?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or felt things that others don’t seem to notice? Or have you believed something strongly that others say isn’t true?\n\nPatient: Seen or heard things? No... no, nothing like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. My hearing is actually quite good for my age, though. Better than my wife's, anyway. She always has the television turned up so loud. She watches those British mystery shows on PBS on Sunday nights. The ones where they live in those grand old manor houses with the sprawling gardens. \n\nI've always wanted a garden like that, with the manicured hedges and the rose bushes. My mother used to grow roses. She had a whole trellis of climbing yellow roses by the back porch. They smelled wonderful in the summer, especially right after a rainstorm. The rain would leave these perfect little droplets on the petals. I used to try and catch the droplets on my finger when I was a boy. We had this big oak tree in the yard, too, and my father hung a tire swing from one of the thickest branches. He used a thick, braided rope he bought from the hardware store downtown. Old Mr. Henderson owned that store. He kept jars of hard candies on the counter for the kids. Butterscotch and peppermint. I always chose the butterscotch. They were wrapped in that crinkly yellow cellophane that made a sharp noise when you twisted it open. It's funny how a sound like that stays with you, isn't it? The crinkle of a candy wrapper. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Something about the television being too loud?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling slowed down or struggling to think clearly, like your thoughts are moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Slowed down... yes. My mind, it's just... I can't seem to hold onto things anymore. That's actually why I came in. I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the youth maker program I volunteer with. It's something I could do in my sleep before, just jotting down a few instructions. But the numbers and words, they just scattered on me. Like trying to catch leaves in the wind. \n\nSpeaking of leaves, the maple out front is already starting to drop its leaves. It seems early this year. Usually, we don't have to start raking until late October. I have this old bamboo rake I've used for decades. The metal ones just tear up the grass, you know? Bamboo is flexible. It glides over the lawn. My grandson, he tried to help me last fall, but he kept swinging the rake like a golf club. He's very into golf right now. Wants to be the next Tiger Woods. I tried playing golf once, back in the eighties. Bought a whole set of clubs at a yard sale. But I could never get the swing right. It always sliced to the right. Right into the woods. Every single time. We spent more time looking for lost balls than actually playing the game. \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? The youth program?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty thinking clearly feel different from how you normally experience grief or stress, and have they been affecting your daily life more than you would expect for someone going through something like this?\n\nPatient: Different? Oh, yes, absolutely. It's not like when my brother passed, or when they closed the plant down. This is... it's heavy. It's ruined everything, really. I just can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I've had to let all my church responsibilities go, and the garden is just a mess of weeds. Even that youth maker program I mentioned... I had to step away from that, too. My wife and our pastor, they've had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. They don't even think it's safe for me to be out in my workshop by myself anymore. \n\nI have a beautiful workshop out back. Built it myself, board by board, back in '98. I used cedar for the siding. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. My grandmother used to have a cedar chest at the foot of her bed. She kept all the winter quilts in it. Heavy, thick quilts she made by hand. One of them had a pattern called the \"Drunkard's Path.\" It's all these curved pieces of fabric. Very difficult to sew. I tried to sew a button on my shirt once, and I pricked my finger so badly it bled on the collar. Had to throw the whole shirt away. It was a nice shirt, too. Flannel. Red and black plaid. Like a lumberjack would wear. I always wanted to go to Maine and see the lumberjacks...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my workshop?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood or energy level changed in the opposite way—like feeling unusually high, overly energetic, or not needing much sleep?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or energetic? No, no, I haven't felt energetic in... well, I can't even remember the last time I had real energy. Definitely not needing less sleep. If anything, I just want to stay in bed all day. My bed is an old four-poster we bought at an antique auction up in Vermont. \n\nWe used to love going to Vermont in the fall. The foliage up there is just spectacular. Reds and oranges that look like the hills are on fire. We'd stay at this little bed and breakfast run by a couple named Arthur and Martha. Arthur made his own maple syrup. Real, thick Vermont maple syrup. Nothing like that watery stuff they sell in the plastic bottles at the grocery store. He tapped the trees himself. It takes something like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup. Can you imagine? Boiling it down for hours and hours. The steam would fill their whole kitchen. It smelled sweet, like burnt sugar. Martha would make these huge stacks of buttermilk pancakes, and we'd eat until we couldn't move. \n\nI tried to make pancakes for my wife a few weeks ago, but I left the burner on too high and set off the smoke alarm. The alarm is one of those new ones that talks to you. It has this calm, robotic woman's voice saying \"Fire. Fire.\" It's very unsettling. I had to get the stepladder out of the garage to turn it off, but I felt so unsteady I almost fell. \n\nWhat was I saying? Did you ask about the smoke alarm?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like woodworking or volunteering, no longer give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, no pleasure at all. I haven't even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. I've skipped choir planning, too, and my regular chess games. Even fishing... fishing just sounds like more effort than it's worth right now, which is really not like me at all. I used to go fishing every single Saturday morning down at Miller's Pond. \n\nThey stock it with trout every spring. Rainbow trout, mostly. Beautiful fish, with that iridescent pink and silver stripe down the side. You have to use the right bait for them, though. A lot of guys swear by those shiny metal spinners, but I always preferred live bait. Nightcrawlers. I'd go out into the yard after a good heavy rain with a flashlight and catch them myself. You have to be quick, grab them right behind the collar, or they zip right back down into their holes. They're surprisingly fast for worms. Earthworms are fascinating creatures, really. They aerate the soil, keep the garden healthy. Charles Darwin actually wrote a whole book about them, you know. He spent his later years just watching worms in his garden...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask me about my garden?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Blaming myself? Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't feel like I'm a burden, exactly. My sister... her passing was just... it was her time, you know? But speaking of my sister, she used to make the most incredible peach cobbler. Every summer, without fail. She had this old cast-iron skillet that belonged to our mother. 

Cast iron is really the best way to bake a cobbler. It heats so evenly. You have to season it, though. A lot of people don't know how to properly season cast iron. You wash it—no soap, never use soap—and then you rub it down with a thin layer of oil and bake it upside down in the oven for an hour. It creates this polymer layer, makes it completely non-stick. I tried to explain that to my nephew once, but he went and put my favorite skillet in the dishwasher. The dishwasher! It came out completely rusted. I spent three hours scrubbing it with steel wool and coarse salt. Salt is amazing for cleaning, actually. People used to use it to preserve meat before refrigeration. They'd pack whole hams in barrels of salt...

Wait, did you ask me about my nephew?

Rented a mixer and everything. He didn't get the ratio of water to powder right, and the whole thing cracked right down the middle after the first frost. Frost heaves are terrible around here. The crinkle of a candy wrapper. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Something about the television being too loud?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling slowed down or struggling to think clearly, like your thoughts are moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Slowed down... yes. My mind, it's just... I can't seem to hold onto things anymore. That's actually why I came in. I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the youth maker program I volunteer with. It's something I could do in my sleep before, just jotting down a few instructions. But the numbers and words, they just scattered on me. Like trying to catch leaves in the wind. \n\nSpeaking of leaves, the maple out front is already starting to drop its leaves. It seems early this year. Usually, we don't have to start raking until late October. I have this old bamboo rake I've used for decades. The metal ones just tear up the grass, you know? Bamboo is flexible. It glides over the lawn. My grandson, he tried to help me last fall, but he kept swinging the rake like a golf club. He's very into golf right now. Wants to be the next Tiger Woods. I tried playing golf once, back in the eighties. Bought a whole set of clubs at a yard sale. But I could never get the swing right. It always sliced to the right. Right into the woods. Every single time. We spent more time looking for lost balls than actually playing the game. \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? The youth program?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty thinking clearly feel different from how you normally experience grief or stress, and have they been affecting your daily life more than you would expect for someone going through something like this?\n\nPatient: Different? Oh, yes, absolutely. It's not like when my brother passed, or when they closed the plant down. This is... it's heavy. It's ruined everything, really. I just can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I've had to let all my church responsibilities go, and the garden is just a mess of weeds. Even that youth maker program I mentioned... I had to step away from that, too. My wife and our pastor, they've had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. They don't even think it's safe for me to be out in my workshop by myself anymore. \n\nI have a beautiful workshop out back. Built it myself, board by board, back in '98. I used cedar for the siding. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. My grandmother used to have a cedar chest at the foot of her bed. She kept all the winter quilts in it. Heavy, thick quilts she made by hand. One of them had a pattern called the \"Drunkard's Path.\" It's all these curved pieces of fabric. Very difficult to sew. I tried to sew a button on my shirt once, and I pricked my finger so badly it bled on the collar. Had to throw the whole shirt away. It was a nice shirt, too. Flannel. Red and black plaid. Like a lumberjack would wear. I always wanted to go to Maine and see the lumberjacks...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my workshop?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood or energy level changed in the opposite way—like feeling unusually high, overly energetic, or not needing much sleep?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or energetic? No, no, I haven't felt energetic in... well, I can't even remember the last time I had real energy. Definitely not needing less sleep. If anything, I just want to stay in bed all day. My bed is an old four-poster we bought at an antique auction up in Vermont. \n\nWe used to love going to Vermont in the fall. The foliage up there is just spectacular. Reds and oranges that look like the hills are on fire. We'd stay at this little bed and breakfast run by a couple named Arthur and Martha. Arthur made his own maple syrup. Real, thick Vermont maple syrup. Nothing like that watery stuff they sell in the plastic bottles at the grocery store. He tapped the trees himself. It takes something like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup. Can you imagine? Boiling it down for hours and hours. The steam would fill their whole kitchen. It smelled sweet, like burnt sugar. Martha would make these huge stacks of buttermilk pancakes, and we'd eat until we couldn't move. \n\nI tried to make pancakes for my wife a few weeks ago, but I left the burner on too high and set off the smoke alarm. The alarm is one of those new ones that talks to you. It has this calm, robotic woman's voice saying \"Fire. Fire.\" It's very unsettling. I had to get the stepladder out of the garage to turn it off, but I felt so unsteady I almost fell. \n\nWhat was I saying? Did you ask about the smoke alarm?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like woodworking or volunteering, no longer give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, no pleasure at all. I haven't even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. I've skipped choir planning, too, and my regular chess games. Even fishing... fishing just sounds like more effort than it's worth right now, which is really not like me at all. I used to go fishing every single Saturday morning down at Miller's Pond. \n\nThey stock it with trout every spring. Rainbow trout, mostly. Beautiful fish, with that iridescent pink and silver stripe down the side. You have to use the right bait for them, though. A lot of guys swear by those shiny metal spinners, but I always preferred live bait. Nightcrawlers. I'd go out into the yard after a good heavy rain with a flashlight and catch them myself. You have to be quick, grab them right behind the collar, or they zip right back down into their holes. They're surprisingly fast for worms. Earthworms are fascinating creatures, really. They aerate the soil, keep the garden healthy. Charles Darwin actually wrote a whole book about them, you know. He spent his later years just watching worms in his garden...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask me about my garden?\n\nDoctor: Have you been blaming yourself for things or feeling like you're a burden to others since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Blaming myself? Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't feel like I'm a burden, exactly. My sister... her passing was just... it was her time, you know? But speaking of my sister, she used to make the most incredible peach cobbler. Every summer, without fail. She had this old cast-iron skillet that belonged to our mother. \n\nCast iron is really the best way to bake a cobbler. It heats so evenly. You have to season it, though. A lot of people don't know how to properly season cast iron. You wash it—no soap, never use soap—and then you rub it down with a thin layer of oil and bake it upside down in the oven for an hour. It creates this polymer layer, makes it completely non-stick. I tried to explain that to my nephew once, but he went and put my favorite skillet in the dishwasher. The dishwasher! It came out completely rusted. I spent three hours scrubbing it with steel wool and coarse salt. Salt is amazing for cleaning, actually. People used to use it to preserve meat before refrigeration. They'd pack whole hams in barrels of salt...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my nephew?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The farmer who owned it was so red in the face when he testified... he wore these thick striped suspenders. You don't see people wearing suspenders much these days, do you? Usually just belts. I have a nice brown leather belt my sister bought me for my birthday a few years ago. She always gave the most practical gifts... \n\nUm. I'm sorry. I'm drifting again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about my wife?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level lately? Like feeling more tired than usual or finding it hard to get through the day?\n\nPatient: Energy? I don't know... I'm not really sure if my energy is different, to be honest. I mean, I suppose I just sit in my chair most of the day now. It's a very comfortable chair, actually. A La-Z-Boy recliner. My wife bought it for me when I retired. It's this deep forest green color, a sort of plush fabric that's incredibly soft. \n\nGreen has always been my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind my childhood home. We used to spend hours back there, my sister and I, building forts out of fallen branches and pretending we were explorers. We'd pack a lunch in an old tin pail—usually peanut butter and jelly sandwiches, cut into triangles, because my sister insisted they tasted better that way. She was very particular about things like that. Even when we were older, she always had to have her tea in a specific floral teacup. It was part of a set she found at an antique shop in Vermont. We took a trip up there one October just to see the foliage. The colors were spectacular... reds and oranges so bright they almost hurt your eyes. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman who made the most incredible blueberry muffins. She said the secret was a dash of nutmeg in the batter. I tried making them once when I got home, but they just didn't turn out the same. Baking is such a precise science, isn't it? You have to measure everything perfectly, or it all falls flat. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask about the chair? I haven't really noticed feeling tired, just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, yes, I suppose that's true. Everything I do has slowed down. Even just getting out of that green chair I mentioned... it takes me forever. It's like my body is moving through wet cement. Even answering your questions feels like my tongue is stuck in cement. \n\nCement is funny stuff, though. You have to mix it just right. My neighbor, Tom, he tried to pour his own patio last summer. Rented a mixer and everything. He didn't get the ratio of water to powder right, and the whole thing cracked right down the middle after the first frost. Frost heaves are terrible around here. They ruin the roads every winter. The city comes around with those patch trucks, throwing cold patch into the potholes, but it never holds. You drive down Elm Street in March, and it's like navigating a minefield. I lost a hubcap on Elm Street once. A shiny chrome one off my old Buick LeSabre. That was a wonderful car. It floated down the highway like a boat. It had those bench seats in the front... you don't see bench seats in cars anymore. Everyone wants bucket seats and center consoles now. My daughter's car has a console that cools your drinks. Can you imagine? A refrigerator right there in the armrest. She always keeps those little cans of sparkling water in it. The grapefruit flavor. It's very tart... \n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? Were we talking about Tom's patio?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or felt things that others don’t seem to notice? Or have you believed something strongly that others say isn’t true?\n\nPatient: Seen or heard things? No... no, nothing like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. My hearing is actually quite good for my age, though. Better than my wife's, anyway. She always has the television turned up so loud. She watches those British mystery shows on PBS on Sunday nights. The ones where they live in those grand old manor houses with the sprawling gardens. \n\nI've always wanted a garden like that, with the manicured hedges and the rose bushes. My mother used to grow roses. She had a whole trellis of climbing yellow roses by the back porch. They smelled wonderful in the summer, especially right after a rainstorm. The rain would leave these perfect little droplets on the petals. I used to try and catch the droplets on my finger when I was a boy. We had this big oak tree in the yard, too, and my father hung a tire swing from one of the thickest branches. He used a thick, braided rope he bought from the hardware store downtown. Old Mr. Henderson owned that store. He kept jars of hard candies on the counter for the kids. Butterscotch and peppermint. I always chose the butterscotch. They were wrapped in that crinkly yellow cellophane that made a sharp noise when you twisted it open. It's funny how a sound like that stays with you, isn't it? The crinkle of a candy wrapper. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Something about the television being too loud?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling slowed down or struggling to think clearly, like your thoughts are moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Slowed down... yes. My mind, it's just... I can't seem to hold onto things anymore. That's actually why I came in. I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the youth maker program I volunteer with. It's something I could do in my sleep before, just jotting down a few instructions. But the numbers and words, they just scattered on me. Like trying to catch leaves in the wind. \n\nSpeaking of leaves, the maple out front is already starting to drop its leaves. It seems early this year. Usually, we don't have to start raking until late October. I have this old bamboo rake I've used for decades. The metal ones just tear up the grass, you know? Bamboo is flexible. It glides over the lawn. My grandson, he tried to help me last fall, but he kept swinging the rake like a golf club. He's very into golf right now. Wants to be the next Tiger Woods. I tried playing golf once, back in the eighties. Bought a whole set of clubs at a yard sale. But I could never get the swing right. It always sliced to the right. Right into the woods. Every single time. We spent more time looking for lost balls than actually playing the game. \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? The youth program?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty thinking clearly feel different from how you normally experience grief or stress, and have they been affecting your daily life more than you would expect for someone going through something like this?\n\nPatient: Different? Oh, yes, absolutely. It's not like when my brother passed, or when they closed the plant down. This is... it's heavy. It's ruined everything, really. I just can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I've had to let all my church responsibilities go, and the garden is just a mess of weeds. Even that youth maker program I mentioned... I had to step away from that, too. My wife and our pastor, they've had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. They don't even think it's safe for me to be out in my workshop by myself anymore. \n\nI have a beautiful workshop out back. Built it myself, board by board, back in '98. I used cedar for the siding. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. My grandmother used to have a cedar chest at the foot of her bed. She kept all the winter quilts in it. Heavy, thick quilts she made by hand. One of them had a pattern called the \"Drunkard's Path.\" It's all these curved pieces of fabric. Very difficult to sew. I tried to sew a button on my shirt once, and I pricked my finger so badly it bled on the collar. Had to throw the whole shirt away. It was a nice shirt, too. Flannel. Red and black plaid. Like a lumberjack would wear. I always wanted to go to Maine and see the lumberjacks...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my workshop?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood or energy level changed in the opposite way—like feeling unusually high, overly energetic, or not needing much sleep?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or energetic? No, no, I haven't felt energetic in... well, I can't even remember the last time I had real energy. Definitely not needing less sleep. If anything, I just want to stay in bed all day. My bed is an old four-poster we bought at an antique auction up in Vermont. \n\nWe used to love going to Vermont in the fall. The foliage up there is just spectacular. Reds and oranges that look like the hills are on fire. We'd stay at this little bed and breakfast run by a couple named Arthur and Martha. Arthur made his own maple syrup. Real, thick Vermont maple syrup. Nothing like that watery stuff they sell in the plastic bottles at the grocery store. He tapped the trees himself. It takes something like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup. Can you imagine? Boiling it down for hours and hours. The steam would fill their whole kitchen. It smelled sweet, like burnt sugar. Martha would make these huge stacks of buttermilk pancakes, and we'd eat until we couldn't move. \n\nI tried to make pancakes for my wife a few weeks ago, but I left the burner on too high and set off the smoke alarm. The alarm is one of those new ones that talks to you. It has this calm, robotic woman's voice saying \"Fire. Fire.\" It's very unsettling. I had to get the stepladder out of the garage to turn it off, but I felt so unsteady I almost fell. \n\nWhat was I saying? Did you ask about the smoke alarm?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like woodworking or volunteering, no longer give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, no pleasure at all. I haven't even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. I've skipped choir planning, too, and my regular chess games. Even fishing... fishing just sounds like more effort than it's worth right now, which is really not like me at all. I used to go fishing every single Saturday morning down at Miller's Pond. \n\nThey stock it with trout every spring. Rainbow trout, mostly. Beautiful fish, with that iridescent pink and silver stripe down the side. You have to use the right bait for them, though. A lot of guys swear by those shiny metal spinners, but I always preferred live bait. Nightcrawlers. I'd go out into the yard after a good heavy rain with a flashlight and catch them myself. You have to be quick, grab them right behind the collar, or they zip right back down into their holes. They're surprisingly fast for worms. Earthworms are fascinating creatures, really. They aerate the soil, keep the garden healthy. Charles Darwin actually wrote a whole book about them, you know. He spent his later years just watching worms in his garden...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask me about my garden?\n\nDoctor: Have you been blaming yourself for things or feeling like you're a burden to others since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Blaming myself? Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't feel like I'm a burden, exactly. My sister... her passing was just... it was her time, you know? But speaking of my sister, she used to make the most incredible peach cobbler. Every summer, without fail. She had this old cast-iron skillet that belonged to our mother. \n\nCast iron is really the best way to bake a cobbler. It heats so evenly. You have to season it, though. A lot of people don't know how to properly season cast iron. You wash it—no soap, never use soap—and then you rub it down with a thin layer of oil and bake it upside down in the oven for an hour. It creates this polymer layer, makes it completely non-stick. I tried to explain that to my nephew once, but he went and put my favorite skillet in the dishwasher. The dishwasher! It came out completely rusted. I spent three hours scrubbing it with steel wool and coarse salt. Salt is amazing for cleaning, actually. People used to use it to preserve meat before refrigeration. They'd pack whole hams in barrels of salt...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my nephew?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Changes in my appetite? Well, yes, I suppose. Food has mostly lost its appeal lately. I just push my breakfast around the plate, and by supper, my wife is reminding me I've barely eaten anything all day. She made eggs this morning. Over easy. I used to love eggs, especially with a little dash of hot sauce. 

There's this hot sauce we found on a trip to Louisiana years ago. Tabasco is fine, but this was a local brand, made with real cayenne peppers they grew right there in their backyard. The guy who made it was named Thibodeaux, I think. Or maybe Boudreaux. One of those classic Cajun names. He wore these bright yellow suspenders and had a hound dog that just slept on the porch all day. Hound dogs are incredible, you know. Their sense of smell is something like ten thousand times stronger than ours. They can track a scent that's days old. The police use them to find missing people. I saw a documentary once about a bloodhound that found a little boy who had wandered into the woods...

They do a lot of robotics and woodworking. My grandfather was a carpenter, actually. He built half the cabinets in my childhood home with his bare hands. The wood was this beautiful cherry oak. You just don't see craftsmanship like that anymore, everything now is just particle board from IKEA... but, um... where was I? \n\nRight, the note. I try to write a simple note for the program, something I could have done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just scatter on me. They just scatter. I can't hold them.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these memory problems? Were they sudden or did they come on gradually over time?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's been about three weeks now. I know exactly when it started because... well, it was right after my older sister passed away. Three weeks ago. She was the one who taught me how to read, actually. We used to sit on the porch of that same house with the cherry oak cabinets, and she'd point out the letters in the Sunday paper. She loved the comics. Peanuts, mostly. I always preferred Calvin and Hobbes, but she said Calvin was too loud. She was always so quiet, you know? Never raised her voice. Not even when she got sick at the end. She just... faded. Like an old photograph left out in the sun. I have a lot of photographs from those days, mostly in black and white. Color film was expensive back then, and my father didn't believe in wasting money on... wait. What were we talking about? The memory thing, right? Yes, about three weeks. Ever since she died.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes in your thinking or memory besides forgetting things? For example, feeling confused, getting lost in familiar places, or having trouble finding the right words when you speak?\n\nPatient: Getting lost? No... no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I know my way around just fine. I still walk down to the corner market, the same route I've taken for thirty years. It used to be owned by the Miller brothers. Good people. Now it's one of those big corporate chains with the bright fluorescent lights that just drain the life right out of you. \n\nThey rearranged all the aisles a few weeks ago, which is frustrating. I was just looking for coffee filters. Just standard basket filters, but they moved them next to the herbal tea. My sister drank tea. Chamomile, usually. She said coffee made her too jittery. We had this old stovetop kettle that whistled so loud... it had a little copper bird on the spout. I haven't heard that whistle in years. I think it got packed away in the attic when I retired. The attic is just full of boxes now. I was an accountant, so you can imagine the paperwork I kept. I used to be so sharp with all those details, balancing everything to the penny... \n\nUm. I'm sorry. What did you ask me? Was it about the grocery store?\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble following conversations, thinking clearly, or keeping track of tasks you need to do?\n\nPatient: Following conversations... yes, that's exactly it. That's what I was trying to say earlier about the youth maker program. I just can't think clearly anymore. I'll sit down to write a simple note for the kids—something I used to do in my sleep—and the numbers and the words, they just scatter. They scatter like... like leaves in the wind. \n\nWe used to have this massive oak tree in the front yard when I was a boy. Every autumn, the yard would just be buried in these crisp, brown leaves. My father would make me rake them into these huge piles. I hated doing it, but I loved jumping into them afterward. The smell of those dry leaves... it's a very specific smell, isn't it? Earthy and kind of sharp. My sister would always scold me because I'd get bits of leaf stuck in my hair, and she was the one who had to help me pick them out before dinner. We always had dinner at exactly six o'clock. Roast beef on Sundays, meatloaf on Tuesdays... my mother was very rigid about the schedule. She believed structure was the key to a good life. But now... \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? The scattering. What was the question? Something about keeping track of things?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy, like the youth maker program or anything else?\n\nPatient: Interest? Well, yes, I suppose. The youth maker program... I haven't even touched the wood pieces on my bench in weeks. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. Dust is funny, isn't it? It just settles everywhere. My mother used to have this feather duster, a real ostrich feather one. She'd go around the house every Saturday morning, flicking it over the lampshades and the picture frames. The dust would just swirl up into the sunlight streaming through the windows... it looked like tiny stars dancing. We had these heavy velvet curtains in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were awful to clean. You had to take them down and haul them to the dry cleaner across town. The man who ran it, Mr. Abernathy, always had a piece of hard candy for me. Butterscotch, usually. I never really liked butterscotch, but I took it to be polite. It's important to be polite, don't you think? \n\nBut yes, the wood pieces... and I've skipped the choir planning committee, too. And my chess games. Even fishing... I used to love going down to the lake early in the morning when the mist was still on the water. But now, it just sounds like so much effort. More effort than it's worth. Which isn't like me at all. I used to be so active. Always doing something. But now... I'm sorry, did that answer your question about the fishing?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day, nearly every day, since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Sad... yes, I suppose that's the word. Most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest. It's just there, the minute I open my eyes. And even when my wife is talking kindly to me, I feel like I'm sitting at the bottom of a well. Just looking up from the dark, completely disconnected. \n\nWe actually had an old stone well on the property where I grew up. My grandfather built it. He laid every single stone by hand. He had these massive, rough hands... he was a stonemason for forty years. He helped build the old courthouse downtown. The one with the big copper clock tower. They don't make buildings like that anymore, with the gargoyles and the real marble floors. Whenever you walked into that courthouse, your footsteps would echo all the way up to the ceiling. I had to go there once for jury duty in the eighties. It was a case about a stolen tractor, if you can believe it. A bright green John Deere. The farmer who owned it was so red in the face when he testified... he wore these thick striped suspenders. You don't see people wearing suspenders much these days, do you? Usually just belts. I have a nice brown leather belt my sister bought me for my birthday a few years ago. She always gave the most practical gifts... \n\nUm. I'm sorry. I'm drifting again, aren't I? What was the question? Something about my wife?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level lately? Like feeling more tired than usual or finding it hard to get through the day?\n\nPatient: Energy? I don't know... I'm not really sure if my energy is different, to be honest. I mean, I suppose I just sit in my chair most of the day now. It's a very comfortable chair, actually. A La-Z-Boy recliner. My wife bought it for me when I retired. It's this deep forest green color, a sort of plush fabric that's incredibly soft. \n\nGreen has always been my favorite color. It reminds me of the woods behind my childhood home. We used to spend hours back there, my sister and I, building forts out of fallen branches and pretending we were explorers. We'd pack a lunch in an old tin pail—usually peanut butter and jelly sandwiches, cut into triangles, because my sister insisted they tasted better that way. She was very particular about things like that. Even when we were older, she always had to have her tea in a specific floral teacup. It was part of a set she found at an antique shop in Vermont. We took a trip up there one October just to see the foliage. The colors were spectacular... reds and oranges so bright they almost hurt your eyes. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman who made the most incredible blueberry muffins. She said the secret was a dash of nutmeg in the batter. I tried making them once when I got home, but they just didn't turn out the same. Baking is such a precise science, isn't it? You have to measure everything perfectly, or it all falls flat. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask about the chair? I haven't really noticed feeling tired, just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, yes, I suppose that's true. Everything I do has slowed down. Even just getting out of that green chair I mentioned... it takes me forever. It's like my body is moving through wet cement. Even answering your questions feels like my tongue is stuck in cement. \n\nCement is funny stuff, though. You have to mix it just right. My neighbor, Tom, he tried to pour his own patio last summer. Rented a mixer and everything. He didn't get the ratio of water to powder right, and the whole thing cracked right down the middle after the first frost. Frost heaves are terrible around here. They ruin the roads every winter. The city comes around with those patch trucks, throwing cold patch into the potholes, but it never holds. You drive down Elm Street in March, and it's like navigating a minefield. I lost a hubcap on Elm Street once. A shiny chrome one off my old Buick LeSabre. That was a wonderful car. It floated down the highway like a boat. It had those bench seats in the front... you don't see bench seats in cars anymore. Everyone wants bucket seats and center consoles now. My daughter's car has a console that cools your drinks. Can you imagine? A refrigerator right there in the armrest. She always keeps those little cans of sparkling water in it. The grapefruit flavor. It's very tart... \n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? Were we talking about Tom's patio?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or felt things that others don’t seem to notice? Or have you believed something strongly that others say isn’t true?\n\nPatient: Seen or heard things? No... no, nothing like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. My hearing is actually quite good for my age, though. Better than my wife's, anyway. She always has the television turned up so loud. She watches those British mystery shows on PBS on Sunday nights. The ones where they live in those grand old manor houses with the sprawling gardens. \n\nI've always wanted a garden like that, with the manicured hedges and the rose bushes. My mother used to grow roses. She had a whole trellis of climbing yellow roses by the back porch. They smelled wonderful in the summer, especially right after a rainstorm. The rain would leave these perfect little droplets on the petals. I used to try and catch the droplets on my finger when I was a boy. We had this big oak tree in the yard, too, and my father hung a tire swing from one of the thickest branches. He used a thick, braided rope he bought from the hardware store downtown. Old Mr. Henderson owned that store. He kept jars of hard candies on the counter for the kids. Butterscotch and peppermint. I always chose the butterscotch. They were wrapped in that crinkly yellow cellophane that made a sharp noise when you twisted it open. It's funny how a sound like that stays with you, isn't it? The crinkle of a candy wrapper. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Something about the television being too loud?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling slowed down or struggling to think clearly, like your thoughts are moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Slowed down... yes. My mind, it's just... I can't seem to hold onto things anymore. That's actually why I came in. I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the youth maker program I volunteer with. It's something I could do in my sleep before, just jotting down a few instructions. But the numbers and words, they just scattered on me. Like trying to catch leaves in the wind. \n\nSpeaking of leaves, the maple out front is already starting to drop its leaves. It seems early this year. Usually, we don't have to start raking until late October. I have this old bamboo rake I've used for decades. The metal ones just tear up the grass, you know? Bamboo is flexible. It glides over the lawn. My grandson, he tried to help me last fall, but he kept swinging the rake like a golf club. He's very into golf right now. Wants to be the next Tiger Woods. I tried playing golf once, back in the eighties. Bought a whole set of clubs at a yard sale. But I could never get the swing right. It always sliced to the right. Right into the woods. Every single time. We spent more time looking for lost balls than actually playing the game. \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? The youth program?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty thinking clearly feel different from how you normally experience grief or stress, and have they been affecting your daily life more than you would expect for someone going through something like this?\n\nPatient: Different? Oh, yes, absolutely. It's not like when my brother passed, or when they closed the plant down. This is... it's heavy. It's ruined everything, really. I just can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I've had to let all my church responsibilities go, and the garden is just a mess of weeds. Even that youth maker program I mentioned... I had to step away from that, too. My wife and our pastor, they've had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. They don't even think it's safe for me to be out in my workshop by myself anymore. \n\nI have a beautiful workshop out back. Built it myself, board by board, back in '98. I used cedar for the siding. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. My grandmother used to have a cedar chest at the foot of her bed. She kept all the winter quilts in it. Heavy, thick quilts she made by hand. One of them had a pattern called the \"Drunkard's Path.\" It's all these curved pieces of fabric. Very difficult to sew. I tried to sew a button on my shirt once, and I pricked my finger so badly it bled on the collar. Had to throw the whole shirt away. It was a nice shirt, too. Flannel. Red and black plaid. Like a lumberjack would wear. I always wanted to go to Maine and see the lumberjacks...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my workshop?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood or energy level changed in the opposite way—like feeling unusually high, overly energetic, or not needing much sleep?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or energetic? No, no, I haven't felt energetic in... well, I can't even remember the last time I had real energy. Definitely not needing less sleep. If anything, I just want to stay in bed all day. My bed is an old four-poster we bought at an antique auction up in Vermont. \n\nWe used to love going to Vermont in the fall. The foliage up there is just spectacular. Reds and oranges that look like the hills are on fire. We'd stay at this little bed and breakfast run by a couple named Arthur and Martha. Arthur made his own maple syrup. Real, thick Vermont maple syrup. Nothing like that watery stuff they sell in the plastic bottles at the grocery store. He tapped the trees himself. It takes something like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup. Can you imagine? Boiling it down for hours and hours. The steam would fill their whole kitchen. It smelled sweet, like burnt sugar. Martha would make these huge stacks of buttermilk pancakes, and we'd eat until we couldn't move. \n\nI tried to make pancakes for my wife a few weeks ago, but I left the burner on too high and set off the smoke alarm. The alarm is one of those new ones that talks to you. It has this calm, robotic woman's voice saying \"Fire. Fire.\" It's very unsettling. I had to get the stepladder out of the garage to turn it off, but I felt so unsteady I almost fell. \n\nWhat was I saying? Did you ask about the smoke alarm?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like woodworking or volunteering, no longer give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, no pleasure at all. I haven't even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. I've skipped choir planning, too, and my regular chess games. Even fishing... fishing just sounds like more effort than it's worth right now, which is really not like me at all. I used to go fishing every single Saturday morning down at Miller's Pond. \n\nThey stock it with trout every spring. Rainbow trout, mostly. Beautiful fish, with that iridescent pink and silver stripe down the side. You have to use the right bait for them, though. A lot of guys swear by those shiny metal spinners, but I always preferred live bait. Nightcrawlers. I'd go out into the yard after a good heavy rain with a flashlight and catch them myself. You have to be quick, grab them right behind the collar, or they zip right back down into their holes. They're surprisingly fast for worms. Earthworms are fascinating creatures, really. They aerate the soil, keep the garden healthy. Charles Darwin actually wrote a whole book about them, you know. He spent his later years just watching worms in his garden...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask me about my garden?\n\nDoctor: Have you been blaming yourself for things or feeling like you're a burden to others since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Blaming myself? Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't feel like I'm a burden, exactly. My sister... her passing was just... it was her time, you know? But speaking of my sister, she used to make the most incredible peach cobbler. Every summer, without fail. She had this old cast-iron skillet that belonged to our mother. \n\nCast iron is really the best way to bake a cobbler. It heats so evenly. You have to season it, though. A lot of people don't know how to properly season cast iron. You wash it—no soap, never use soap—and then you rub it down with a thin layer of oil and bake it upside down in the oven for an hour. It creates this polymer layer, makes it completely non-stick. I tried to explain that to my nephew once, but he went and put my favorite skillet in the dishwasher. The dishwasher! It came out completely rusted. I spent three hours scrubbing it with steel wool and coarse salt. Salt is amazing for cleaning, actually. People used to use it to preserve meat before refrigeration. They'd pack whole hams in barrels of salt...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my nephew?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Changes in my appetite? Well, yes, I suppose. Food has mostly lost its appeal lately. I just push my breakfast around the plate, and by supper, my wife is reminding me I've barely eaten anything all day. She made eggs this morning. Over easy. I used to love eggs, especially with a little dash of hot sauce. \n\nThere's this hot sauce we found on a trip to Louisiana years ago. Tabasco is fine, but this was a local brand, made with real cayenne peppers they grew right there in their backyard. The guy who made it was named Thibodeaux, I think. Or maybe Boudreaux. One of those classic Cajun names. He wore these bright yellow suspenders and had a hound dog that just slept on the porch all day. Hound dogs are incredible, you know. Their sense of smell is something like ten thousand times stronger than ours. They can track a scent that's days old. The police use them to find missing people. I saw a documentary once about a bloodhound that found a little boy who had wandered into the woods... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we discussing? Did you ask about the dog?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
