We took a trip up there one October just to see the foliage. The colors were spectacular... reds and oranges so bright they almost hurt your eyes. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast run by a woman who made the most incredible blueberry muffins. She said the secret was a dash of nutmeg in the batter. I tried making them once when I got home, but they just didn't turn out the same. Baking is such a precise science, isn't it? You have to measure everything perfectly, or it all falls flat. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask about the chair? I haven't really noticed feeling tired, just... I don't know.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, like you feel restless inside?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Well, yes, I suppose that's true. Everything I do has slowed down. Even just getting out of that green chair I mentioned... it takes me forever. It's like my body is moving through wet cement. Even answering your questions feels like my tongue is stuck in cement. \n\nCement is funny stuff, though. You have to mix it just right. My neighbor, Tom, he tried to pour his own patio last summer. Rented a mixer and everything. He didn't get the ratio of water to powder right, and the whole thing cracked right down the middle after the first frost. Frost heaves are terrible around here. They ruin the roads every winter. The city comes around with those patch trucks, throwing cold patch into the potholes, but it never holds. You drive down Elm Street in March, and it's like navigating a minefield. I lost a hubcap on Elm Street once. A shiny chrome one off my old Buick LeSabre. That was a wonderful car. It floated down the highway like a boat. It had those bench seats in the front... you don't see bench seats in cars anymore. Everyone wants bucket seats and center consoles now. My daughter's car has a console that cools your drinks. Can you imagine? A refrigerator right there in the armrest. She always keeps those little cans of sparkling water in it. The grapefruit flavor. It's very tart... \n\nI'm sorry, I've lost the thread again, haven't I? Were we talking about Tom's patio?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen, heard, or felt things that others don’t seem to notice? Or have you believed something strongly that others say isn’t true?\n\nPatient: Seen or heard things? No... no, nothing like that. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. My hearing is actually quite good for my age, though. Better than my wife's, anyway. She always has the television turned up so loud. She watches those British mystery shows on PBS on Sunday nights. The ones where they live in those grand old manor houses with the sprawling gardens. \n\nI've always wanted a garden like that, with the manicured hedges and the rose bushes. My mother used to grow roses. She had a whole trellis of climbing yellow roses by the back porch. They smelled wonderful in the summer, especially right after a rainstorm. The rain would leave these perfect little droplets on the petals. I used to try and catch the droplets on my finger when I was a boy. We had this big oak tree in the yard, too, and my father hung a tire swing from one of the thickest branches. He used a thick, braided rope he bought from the hardware store downtown. Old Mr. Henderson owned that store. He kept jars of hard candies on the counter for the kids. Butterscotch and peppermint. I always chose the butterscotch. They were wrapped in that crinkly yellow cellophane that made a sharp noise when you twisted it open. It's funny how a sound like that stays with you, isn't it? The crinkle of a candy wrapper. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Something about the television being too loud?\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling slowed down or struggling to think clearly, like your thoughts are moving through molasses?\n\nPatient: Slowed down... yes. My mind, it's just... I can't seem to hold onto things anymore. That's actually why I came in. I sat down the other day to write a simple note for the youth maker program I volunteer with. It's something I could do in my sleep before, just jotting down a few instructions. But the numbers and words, they just scattered on me. Like trying to catch leaves in the wind. \n\nSpeaking of leaves, the maple out front is already starting to drop its leaves. It seems early this year. Usually, we don't have to start raking until late October. I have this old bamboo rake I've used for decades. The metal ones just tear up the grass, you know? Bamboo is flexible. It glides over the lawn. My grandson, he tried to help me last fall, but he kept swinging the rake like a golf club. He's very into golf right now. Wants to be the next Tiger Woods. I tried playing golf once, back in the eighties. Bought a whole set of clubs at a yard sale. But I could never get the swing right. It always sliced to the right. Right into the woods. Every single time. We spent more time looking for lost balls than actually playing the game. \n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? The youth program?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty thinking clearly feel different from how you normally experience grief or stress, and have they been affecting your daily life more than you would expect for someone going through something like this?\n\nPatient: Different? Oh, yes, absolutely. It's not like when my brother passed, or when they closed the plant down. This is... it's heavy. It's ruined everything, really. I just can't do the things I'm supposed to do. I've had to let all my church responsibilities go, and the garden is just a mess of weeds. Even that youth maker program I mentioned... I had to step away from that, too. My wife and our pastor, they've had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. They don't even think it's safe for me to be out in my workshop by myself anymore. \n\nI have a beautiful workshop out back. Built it myself, board by board, back in '98. I used cedar for the siding. Cedar smells so good when you cut it, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. My grandmother used to have a cedar chest at the foot of her bed. She kept all the winter quilts in it. Heavy, thick quilts she made by hand. One of them had a pattern called the \"Drunkard's Path.\" It's all these curved pieces of fabric. Very difficult to sew. I tried to sew a button on my shirt once, and I pricked my finger so badly it bled on the collar. Had to throw the whole shirt away. It was a nice shirt, too. Flannel. Red and black plaid. Like a lumberjack would wear. I always wanted to go to Maine and see the lumberjacks...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my workshop?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood or energy level changed in the opposite way—like feeling unusually high, overly energetic, or not needing much sleep?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or energetic? No, no, I haven't felt energetic in... well, I can't even remember the last time I had real energy. Definitely not needing less sleep. If anything, I just want to stay in bed all day. My bed is an old four-poster we bought at an antique auction up in Vermont. \n\nWe used to love going to Vermont in the fall. The foliage up there is just spectacular. Reds and oranges that look like the hills are on fire. We'd stay at this little bed and breakfast run by a couple named Arthur and Martha. Arthur made his own maple syrup. Real, thick Vermont maple syrup. Nothing like that watery stuff they sell in the plastic bottles at the grocery store. He tapped the trees himself. It takes something like forty gallons of sap just to make one gallon of syrup. Can you imagine? Boiling it down for hours and hours. The steam would fill their whole kitchen. It smelled sweet, like burnt sugar. Martha would make these huge stacks of buttermilk pancakes, and we'd eat until we couldn't move. \n\nI tried to make pancakes for my wife a few weeks ago, but I left the burner on too high and set off the smoke alarm. The alarm is one of those new ones that talks to you. It has this calm, robotic woman's voice saying \"Fire. Fire.\" It's very unsettling. I had to get the stepladder out of the garage to turn it off, but I felt so unsteady I almost fell. \n\nWhat was I saying? Did you ask about the smoke alarm?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like woodworking or volunteering, no longer give you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Pleasure? No, no pleasure at all. I haven't even touched the wood pieces sitting on my bench. They're just sitting there, gathering dust. I've skipped choir planning, too, and my regular chess games. Even fishing... fishing just sounds like more effort than it's worth right now, which is really not like me at all. I used to go fishing every single Saturday morning down at Miller's Pond. \n\nThey stock it with trout every spring. Rainbow trout, mostly. Beautiful fish, with that iridescent pink and silver stripe down the side. You have to use the right bait for them, though. A lot of guys swear by those shiny metal spinners, but I always preferred live bait. Nightcrawlers. I'd go out into the yard after a good heavy rain with a flashlight and catch them myself. You have to be quick, grab them right behind the collar, or they zip right back down into their holes. They're surprisingly fast for worms. Earthworms are fascinating creatures, really. They aerate the soil, keep the garden healthy. Charles Darwin actually wrote a whole book about them, you know. He spent his later years just watching worms in his garden...\n\nI'm sorry, what were we talking about? Did you ask me about my garden?\n\nDoctor: Have you been blaming yourself for things or feeling like you're a burden to others since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Blaming myself? Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't feel like I'm a burden, exactly. My sister... her passing was just... it was her time, you know? But speaking of my sister, she used to make the most incredible peach cobbler. Every summer, without fail. She had this old cast-iron skillet that belonged to our mother. \n\nCast iron is really the best way to bake a cobbler. It heats so evenly. You have to season it, though. A lot of people don't know how to properly season cast iron. You wash it—no soap, never use soap—and then you rub it down with a thin layer of oil and bake it upside down in the oven for an hour. It creates this polymer layer, makes it completely non-stick. I tried to explain that to my nephew once, but he went and put my favorite skillet in the dishwasher. The dishwasher! It came out completely rusted. I spent three hours scrubbing it with steel wool and coarse salt. Salt is amazing for cleaning, actually. People used to use it to preserve meat before refrigeration. They'd pack whole hams in barrels of salt...\n\nWait, did you ask me about my nephew?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Changes in my appetite? Well, yes, I suppose. Food has mostly lost its appeal lately. I just push my breakfast around the plate, and by supper, my wife is reminding me I've barely eaten anything all day. She made eggs this morning. Over easy. I used to love eggs, especially with a little dash of hot sauce. \n\nThere's this hot sauce we found on a trip to Louisiana years ago. Tabasco is fine, but this was a local brand, made with real cayenne peppers they grew right there in their backyard. The guy who made it was named Thibodeaux, I think. Or maybe Boudreaux. One of those classic Cajun names. He wore these bright yellow suspenders and had a hound dog that just slept on the porch all day. Hound dogs are incredible, you know. Their sense of smell is something like ten thousand times stronger than ours. They can track a scent that's days old. The police use them to find missing people. I saw a documentary once about a bloodhound that found a little boy who had wandered into the woods... \n\nI'm sorry, what were we discussing? Did you ask about the dog?"
  }
